\part{Assembly, and what the computer checked}
%======================================================================

\section{Assembly: proof of the dichotomy}\label{sec:assembly-friendly}

All the pieces exist; this section only has to bolt them together.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:main-friendly}]
\emph{Part (1), existence.} This is Theorem~\ref{thm:existence-friendly} (proved
there for all $k \ge 2$, hence for the problem's $k \ge 3$): if $d_2 \ge k+1$,
the announcement $r = 192\,d_2$ wins against every lacunary $B$ of that ratio.

\emph{Part (2), strong non-existence.} Let $d_2 \le k$ (with $k \ge 3$) and let
$r_1, r_2, \dots$ be any prescribed sequence of ratio lower bounds. Build the
certificate $B$ of Lemma~\ref{lem:cert-friendly} for the sequence $R(i) := r_i$;
it satisfies $b_{i+1} \ge r_i\,b_i$ for every $i$. Let $A$ be any
$(d_1,d_2)$-sequence. By Proposition~\ref{prop:goal} --- now unconditional, since
Part~IV proved SHARP (Theorem~\ref{thm:sharp-friendly}) --- $A$ is
tail-covering. Retracing which case fired, for the record:

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule
The walk's tail alphabet (rescaled) & Case closed by \\
\midrule
one letter & Lemma~\ref{lem:oneletter-friendly} \\
two letters, gap word eventually periodic & Lemma~\ref{lem:periodic-friendly} \\
two letters, $k$ even and $d_2 = k$ & Proposition~\ref{prop:boundary-friendly} \\
two letters, some $W_2 \ge 2$ ($k$ odd or $d_2 \le k{-}1$) &
  Lemmas~\ref{lem:width-friendly}(a), \ref{lem:sweep-friendly} \\
two letters, balanced & Theorem~\ref{thm:mh-friendly}, then
  Lemma~\ref{lem:periodic-friendly} or \ref{lem:sturmian-friendly} \\
three or more letters & Lemma~\ref{lem:slot-friendly} $+$ SHARP \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

By Lemma~\ref{lem:cert-friendly}(2), $(kA) \cap B \ne \emptyset$. As $A$ was
arbitrary, the single $B$ defeats every admissible walk; as $(r_i)$ was
arbitrary, this is the strong form.

\emph{The dichotomy.} For any $k \ge 3$ and $1 \le d_1 < d_2$, exactly one of
$d_2 \ge k + 1$ and $d_2 \le k$ holds; part (1) gives a winning strategy in the
first case, part (2) denies one in the second (a winning $r$ would in particular
defeat the certificate built for the constant sequence $r_i \equiv r$). So the
answer to Erd\H{o}s problem 1112 is: \emph{yes precisely when $d_2 \ge k+1$}.
\end{proof}

Every promise of the honest ledger (\S\ref{sec:honest-list}) has now been
redeemed: gap (1) in Part~II, gaps (2) and (3) in Part~III, gap (4) in Part~IV.
The remaining section is not part of the proof; it is about how the proof is
\emph{checked}.

\begin{checkpoint}[\textsc{challenge} --- grand tour]\label{chk:grand-tour}
Fix $k = 3$, $(d_1, d_2) = (1, 3)$ --- the losing parameter set of
\S\ref{sec:map-friendly} --- and the ratio sequence $r_i = 10^i$. Follow
the entire non-existence machine for the specific walk with gap word
$1,2,1,1,2,1,2,\dots$ (the Fibonacci word's letters $+1$): which case of the
table fires, what modulus results, and where in the certificate $B$'s
construction does the collision get guaranteed? (Nothing new to prove --- the
point is to watch every part of Parts~III--IV touch one concrete example.)
\end{checkpoint}

%======================================================================

\section{What the computer checked, and how you can too}\label{sec:computer}

This proof is computer-assisted, and this paper has flagged every place the
assistance occurs: the $172$ Table-A certificates and the $a \le 3000$ scan
(Case T), and the $178$ Table-B base certificates with the class enumeration
(Case B) --- those four items are the load-bearing finite layer. (The parent
paper's harnesses additionally run corroborating enumerations that this
document's proofs never lean on, such as listing all minimal alphabets with
$M \le 130$; corroboration is welcome, but the ledger above is complete without
it.) This
closing section explains what, exactly, was computed, why you need not take
anyone's word for it --- and what such checking can and cannot promise. Nothing
here is needed to \emph{understand} the proof; it is needed to \emph{trust} it,
which is a different and equally honorable activity.

\subsection{Certificates, not searches}

Every computed object in this work is a \emph{certificate}: a small tuple whose
correctness is a finite, mindless computation. A Table-A or Table-B row
$(a, b, M;\ x, Y, Z)$ asserts two things --- the multiset of $x$ copies of $a$,
$Y$ of $b$, $Z$ of $M$ has size $\le M - 1$, and its subset sums contain $M$
consecutive integers --- and \emph{you} can check any row with pencil and paper
in minutes (Exercises in \S\S\ref{sec:caseT-friendly}--\ref{sec:caseB-friendly}
did exactly this). How the rows were \emph{found} is irrelevant to their truth:
a certificate severs the proof from the search that produced it. The same holds
for the scans: ``this formula exceeds that bound at exactly these $172$ inputs''
is a statement any programmer can re-derive in an afternoon, in any language.

To be precise about packaging: the complete human-readable proof record is
\emph{this paper alone}. All $350$ rows are printed in
Appendix~\ref{app:tables}, generated from the same canonical data that the Lean
kernel independently decides, and exported as CSV with recorded digests for
readers who would rather compute than read. Nothing load-bearing is held
elsewhere.

\subsection{Three independent checks}

The repository verifies the finite layer three separate ways, sharing no
code:

\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Two Python harnesses.} One reconstructs every designated witness
      across all $83{,}251$ hard-core triples with $M \le 120$ and verifies each
      in exact integer arithmetic; the other is a from-scratch re-implementation
      working only from the lemma statements. Both fail closed --- any invalid
      row, missing class, or deviant count aborts with an error --- and both
      report identical branch tallies ($2392$ D, $2605$ P, $1420$ L, $71{,}421$
      E, $3254 + 172$ T, $1987$ B) --- tallies which sum to exactly $83{,}251$,
      the number of triples: the books visibly close. Agreement between
      independent implementations is strong evidence against ordinary coding
      slips --- two people are unlikely to mistype the same thing --- but it is
      evidence, not proof, and it says nothing at all about a shared
      misunderstanding of the mathematical design, since both programs were
      written from the same case analysis. That is what the proofs are for.
\item \emph{The table generator.} The typeset tables in the certificate
      supplement are \emph{generated} from one canonical data file by a script
      that re-verifies every row (budget and coverage, by exact bitmask
      arithmetic) on every build, so no printed row can silently diverge from
      the data that is checked.
\item \emph{The Lean kernel.} The entire proof --- not just the finite layer ---
      is formalized in Lean~4 and machine-checked; the certificate tables are
      transcribed into the formal development and re-decided by Lean's kernel.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{What a Lean formalization is}

Lean is a \emph{proof assistant}: a programming language in which definitions,
theorem statements and proofs are all written formally, and a small program ---
the \emph{kernel} --- checks that every proof step follows from the rules of
logic and the axioms. The human (or, in this work, largely the AI systems noted
below) supplies the proof; the kernel is the referee that cannot be sweet-talked.
Three facts summarize the state of this development:

\begin{itemize}
\item It is \emph{complete}: all three headline theorems --- the dichotomy, the
      explicit ratio $192\,d_2$, and the strong non-existence form --- are
      proved, over frozen definitions, from start to finish.
\item It is \emph{gap-free}: no \texttt{sorry} (Lean's ``I owe you a proof''
      placeholder) anywhere, and the axiom audit reports only Lean's three
      standard foundational axioms.
\item Its finite checks run \emph{in the kernel}: no appeal to compiled
      external code (no \texttt{native\_decide}), so even the $172 + 178$
      certificate rows and the $a \le 3000$ scan are validated by the same
      small trusted checker as every logical step.
\end{itemize}

Anyone can reproduce this: clone the repository, run \texttt{lake build}, and
watch it compile with no complaints (the repository README gives the exact
commands, and links a public log of a complete run).

\subsection{What machine-checking does not promise}

Honesty requires the other half. A formal verification establishes: \emph{these
formal statements follow from these axioms}. It does not establish:

\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{That the formal statement means what the English problem says.} If
      the Lean definition of ``lacunary'' or ``gap window'' subtly mismatched
      the problem, the kernel would happily verify the wrong theorem. This
      correspondence must be audited by humans reading both --- which is why
      \S\ref{sec:faithful} walks through each modelling
      choice (for instance: over the natural numbers, careless subtraction
      truncates at zero, and a naively phrased gap condition would silently
      admit decreasing walks; the formalization phrases the window additively
      to dodge exactly this). The definitions are few and short by design, to
      make that audit as small as possible.
\item \emph{Novelty or attribution.} Whether SHARP is genuinely new is a
      question about the literature, addressed in \S\ref{sec:prior} by a
      documented search --- no computer can settle it.
\item \emph{That nothing outside the trusted base is wrong.} The kernel, the
      pinned compiler and library versions --- these are trusted. The trust
      base is small and standard, but it is not zero; no honest account says
      otherwise.
\end{itemize}

The layers of trust, drawn as a stack --- each layer checks the one above and
must itself be trusted or audited:

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[y=0.72cm, line width=0.4pt]
  \foreach \y/\txt/\note in {
    4/{English theorem (Erd\H{o}s 1112)}/{faithfulness: human-audited, \S\ref{sec:computer}},
    3/{formal statements (frozen Lean definitions)}/{small by design, so the audit is small},
    2/{the Lean development (every proof step)}/{checked mechanically by the kernel},
    1/{kernel $+$ three standard axioms}/{trusted --- small, standard, but not zero},
    0/{certificates $+$ two Python harnesses}/{independent corroboration, outside the stack}}
  {
    \node[draw, rounded corners, minimum width=6.4cm, minimum height=0.55cm,
          font=\scriptsize] (b\y) at (0,\y) {\txt};
    \node[font=\tiny, black!55, right=2mm of b\y] {\note};
  }
  \foreach \a/\b in {4/3, 3/2, 2/1}
    \draw[-{Stealth}] (b\a.south) -- (b\b.north);
  \draw[densely dotted, -{Stealth}] (b0.north) -- (b1.south);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

One story worth retelling: an early draft of the
staircase lemma omitted the frame-merge hypothesis, and the \emph{formalization
process} caught it --- the counterexample $(5, 7, 9)$ of Remark~\ref{rem:579}
is now permanently enshrined in the development as a guard. The moral runs both
ways: human-written mathematics slips, and machine-checking catches real slips
--- and what the machine certifies is exactly the statement it was given,
neither more nor less.

\subsection{The formal statement, and why it is faithful}\label{sec:faithful}

A formal proof certifies only the statement it was given, so the statement
deserves to be read. The definitions live in a frozen file
(\texttt{lean/Erdos1112.lean}) that the development imports but never edits, and
the three theorems are stated once each, directly over those definitions:

\begin{quote}\small
\begin{alltt}
def IsLacunaryWith (r : ℕ) (b : ℕ → ℕ) : Prop :=
  0 < b 0 ∧ StrictMono b ∧ ∀ i, r * b i ≤ b (i + 1)

def HasGapsIn (d₁ d₂ : ℕ) (a : ℕ → ℕ) : Prop :=
  0 < a 0 ∧ ∀ i, a i + d₁ ≤ a (i + 1) ∧ a (i + 1) ≤ a i + d₂

def kFoldSumset (k : ℕ) (a : ℕ → ℕ) : Set ℕ :=
  \{ n | ∃ f : Fin k → ℕ, n = ∑ j, a (f j) \}

def RatioWorks (k d₁ d₂ r : ℕ) : Prop :=
  ∀ b : ℕ → ℕ, IsLacunaryWith r b →
    ∃ a : ℕ → ℕ, HasGapsIn d₁ d₂ a ∧
      Disjoint (kFoldSumset k a) (Set.range b)

def Question (k d₁ d₂ : ℕ) : Prop := ∃ r : ℕ, RatioWorks k d₁ d₂ r
\end{alltt}
\end{quote}

Six modelling choices carry the weight, and each is a place where a formalization
could quietly prove the wrong theorem.

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Sets as increasing enumerations.} $A$ and $B$ are infinite sets of
positive integers, encoded by the functions that list them in order.
\texttt{StrictMono} is what makes such a function enumerate a \emph{set} rather
than a multiset; it is imposed on $B$ directly and \emph{derived} for $A$ from the
gap condition when $d_1 \ge 1$.

\item \textbf{The gap window, phrased additively.} Over the natural numbers,
subtraction is truncated at zero --- so a subtractive phrasing would be a trap. An
isolated upper bound ``$a_{i+1} - a_i \le d_2$'' would read $0 \le d_2$ on a
\emph{decreasing} step, which is true, and would silently admit walks that go
backwards. Writing the window as $a_i + d_1 \le a_{i+1} \wedge a_{i+1} \le a_i +
d_2$ avoids this entirely. (The lower bound is not the trap: an underflowed gap
gives $d_1 \le 0$, false, since $d_1 \ge 1$.)

\item \textbf{The sumset, with repetition.} \texttt{kFoldSumset} ranges over an
arbitrary index function $f$ with \emph{no} injectivity requirement, so summands
may repeat --- the intended $kA$ of Definition~\ref{def:sumset}. Requiring $f$
injective would model the distinct-summand sumset, a strictly smaller set and a
different problem.

\item \textbf{Quantifier order.} \texttt{Question} is $\exists r\ \forall B\
\exists A$, with $A$ chosen after $B$ --- the game of \S\ref{sec:game}, not the
easier $\exists A\ \forall B$.

\item \textbf{The ratio over $\NN$ rather than $\ZZ$.} The problem says ``an
integer $r$''. Passing to $\NN$ loses no existence content, and this is now proved
rather than argued: the frozen file also carries the integer form
\texttt{QuestionInt} together with the machine-checked equivalence
\texttt{question\_iff\_questionInt}, and the headline theorem in integer form
\texttt{erdos\_1112\_int}. So the dichotomy is verified for the problem's literal
phrasing.

\item \textbf{A genuine, non-vacuous biconditional.} Both directions are separately
witnessed, and both sides are inhabited: none of the predicates is silently
trivial. \texttt{IsLacunaryWith r} is satisfiable for every $r$ (take $b_i =
(r+2)^i$), \texttt{HasGapsIn} is satisfiable (take $a_i = 1 + d_1 i$), and
\texttt{kFoldSumset k a} is nonempty while \texttt{Set.range b} is infinite --- so
disjointness is a real constraint, not a vacuous one.
\end{enumerate}

\emph{Scope.} Every headline theorem carries the problem's own hypothesis $k \ge
3$. The existence half is proved here in prose for all $k \ge 2$
(\S\ref{sec:beatty}); the formalization deliberately carries $k \ge 3$ throughout,
so the $k = 2$ existence statement stands as an unformalized supplementary
observation. Nothing in the mathematical argument of this paper is delegated to
the formalization.

\emph{Trust base.} At the commit recorded in \S\ref{sec:data}, built with the
pinned toolchain \texttt{leanprover/lean4:v4.27.0} and the Mathlib revision fixed
by \texttt{lake-manifest.json}, \texttt{\#print axioms} reports for each of the
three headline theorems exactly
\[
  \texttt{[propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound]}
\]
--- Lean's three standard foundational axioms, and nothing else. In particular
there is no \texttt{sorryAx} (no gaps) and no \texttt{Lean.ofReduceBool} (the axiom
\texttt{native\_decide} would introduce). Every finite check, including the $350$
certificate rows and the $a \le 3000$ scan, is therefore discharged by the trusted
kernel rather than by compiled code. The audit is itself the gap check: a
\texttt{sorry} anywhere would surface in that list.

\subsection{What machine-checking still does not settle}

Two honest caveats. ``Depends only on three axioms'' is a statement about the
\emph{logical} axiom report; the operative trusted base is larger, and includes the
Lean kernel and elaborator, the Mathlib source at the pinned revision, and the
dependency snapshot. And an axiom audit cannot detect a mismatch between a numbered
result in this paper and the declaration that purports to formalize it. That
correspondence is asserted in \S\ref{app:lean} and must be checked by reading both
--- which is one more reason for a paper written to be read.

\section{Provenance and authorship}\label{sec:provenance}

This work was produced with substantial assistance from automated systems, and we
record the division of labour.

The mathematical constructions, case analyses and proofs, together with the Lean
formalization and a first draft of this paper, were produced by Claude (Anthropic;
models Fable~5, Opus~4.8 and Opus~5) acting as the reasoning agent, under the
author's direction. GPT-5.5 (OpenAI) and Gemini~3.1 (Google DeepMind) were used for
advice and for adversarial review of drafts.

The author selected the problem and fixed its formalization; set the intermediate
targets and overall strategy; decided which candidate routes to pursue and which to
abandon; audited each construction and proof; chose the formal statement; and made
the final mathematical judgements. He takes full responsibility for the content,
including any error that remains. The automated systems are tools, not authors:
they meet no authorship criterion, cannot take responsibility for the work, and are
not listed as authors.

Formal verification settles correctness relative to the formal statement only; it
does not establish novelty, attribution, or exposition. The one thing no
formalization can settle on the reader's behalf --- that the formal statement means
what the informal problem says --- is set out decision by decision in
\S\ref{sec:faithful}.

If you are a student reading this: the theorem is true because of the proofs you
have just read and can check --- every one of them, with enough patience, by hand
--- and not because any person or machine says so. That is the whole point of
mathematics, and it is why a paper like this one can exist at all.

\section{Data availability and reproduction}\label{sec:data}

Everything needed to check this paper is permanently archived at
\[
  \texttt{doi:10.5281/zenodo.21568276}
\]
--- release \texttt{v1.0.0}, deposited with CERN's Zenodo repository under the
Apache-2.0 licence. That snapshot carries this \LaTeX{} source, the Lean~4
development \cite{Lean4,Mathlib}, the machine-readable certificate exports
(\texttt{data/table-A.csv}, \texttt{data/table-B.csv}, \texttt{data/SHA256SUMS}),
the table generator and re-verifier \texttt{gen-tables.py}, the two independent
Python harnesses, and the documented prior-art search
(\texttt{paper/novelty-search.md}).

The identifier above resolves to that exact snapshot and will not change; the
concept identifier \texttt{doi:10.5281/zenodo.21568275} resolves instead to the
most recent version, should later ones exist. Development continues in the open
at \repo, but no claim in this paper depends on that repository remaining
available.

\emph{The machine-checked proof.} A clean checkout builds with the pinned toolchain:
\begin{quote}\small
\begin{verbatim}
lake exe cache get                            # fetch the pinned Mathlib
lake build                                    # compile every file
lake env lean Erdos1112Proof/AxiomsCheck.lean # the 3-axiom report, per theorem
\end{verbatim}
\end{quote}
Continuous integration re-runs the pinned build, the axiom audit, the table
re-verification and the correspondence check of \S\ref{app:lean} on every push.

\emph{The finite layer.} The complete certificate tables are typeset in the
Appendix~\ref{app:tables} and exported as CSV with recorded SHA-256 digests. Running \texttt{make} regenerates both from the one
canonical source and re-checks every row (budget $\le M-1$, and coverage of $M$
consecutive integers, by exact bitmask arithmetic, aborting on any failure), so no
listing --- printed, exported, or kernel-decided --- can silently diverge from the
others. Any single row can also be checked by hand in a few minutes, by the method
of \S\ref{sec:computer}.

%======================================================================
\section{Every expansion, in full}\label{app:algebra}

The main text keeps ideas in the foreground and refers routine expansions here.
Each item is pure algebra --- no cleverness, only care.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{(A1) Lemma~\ref{lem:L1-friendly}: the run has length $\ge M$.}
With $x = M - \alpha$, $S = (\alpha-1)\gamma$ and $\gamma \le M - \alpha - 1$:
\begin{align*}
  \alpha x - S + 1
  &= \alpha(M - \alpha) - (\alpha - 1)\gamma + 1 \\
  &\ge \alpha(M-\alpha) - (\alpha-1)(M - \alpha - 1) + 1 \\
  &= \big[ \alpha M - \alpha^2 \big] - \big[ \alpha M - \alpha^2 - M + 1 \big] + 1
  && \text{since } (\alpha-1)(M-\alpha-1) = \alpha M - \alpha^2 - M + 1 \\
  &= M .
\end{align*}
(The middle expansion: $(\alpha - 1)(M - \alpha - 1) = \alpha M - \alpha^2 -
\alpha - M + \alpha + 1 = \alpha M - \alpha^2 - M + 1$.)

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{(A2) Lemma~\ref{lem:L2-friendly}: the covered length.}
With $x = \beta - 1$, $y = \alpha - 1 + t$, $C = (\alpha-1)(\beta-1) = \alpha\beta
- \alpha - \beta + 1$:
\begin{align*}
  \alpha x + \beta y - 2C + 1
  &= \alpha(\beta - 1) + \beta(\alpha - 1 + t) - 2(\alpha\beta - \alpha - \beta + 1) + 1 \\
  &= \alpha\beta - \alpha + \alpha\beta - \beta + t\beta - 2\alpha\beta + 2\alpha
     + 2\beta - 2 + 1 \\
  &= \alpha + \beta - 1 + t\beta .
\end{align*}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{(A3) Lemma~\ref{lem:L3-friendly}: the worst-case rearrangement.}
At $M = d\beta + 1$: $y = M - d - \beta + 1 = (d-1)(\beta - 1) + 1$. Then, with
$C = (\alpha-1)(\beta-1)$,
\begin{align*}
  L &= \alpha x + \beta y - 2C + 1
     = \alpha(\beta-1) + \beta\big[(d-1)(\beta-1) + 1\big] - 2(\alpha-1)(\beta-1) + 1 \\
    &= (\beta - 1)\big[ \alpha + \beta(d-1) - 2\alpha + 2 \big] + \beta + 1
     = (\beta-1)\big[ \beta(d-1) - \alpha + 2 \big] + \beta + 1 .
\end{align*}
The claim $L \ge M + 1 = d\beta + 2$ is therefore
\[
  (\beta-1)\big[ \beta(d-1) - \alpha + 2 \big] \;\ge\; d\beta + 2 - \beta - 1
  \;=\; \beta(d-1) + 1 ,
\]
i.e.\ $(\beta - 1)(u - \alpha + 2) \ge u + 1$ with $u := \beta(d-1)$, as stated.
At $d = 2$ ($u = \beta$): expand $(\beta-1)(\beta - \alpha + 2) = \beta^2 -
\alpha\beta + 2\beta - \beta + \alpha - 2$, so the claim is $\beta^2 - \alpha\beta
+ \beta + \alpha - 2 \ge \beta + 1$, i.e.\ $\beta(\beta - \alpha) + \alpha - 3 \ge
0$. Monotonicity in $u$: the difference $(\beta-1)(u - \alpha + 2) - (u+1)$ has
$u$-coefficient $(\beta - 1) - 1 = \beta - 2 \ge 0$ for $\beta \ge 2$; and if
$\beta = 2$ then $\alpha = 1$ and the difference is $(u + 1) - (u + 1) = 0$ ---
equality, which suffices.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{(A4) Lemma~\ref{lem:L3-friendly}: the bridge length.}
\[
  (L-1)d - (d-1)M + 1 \ge M
  \iff (L-1)d \ge dM - 1
  \iff L \ge M + 1 - \tfrac1d .
\]
Since $L$ is an integer and $0 < \tfrac1d < 1$, the number $M + 1 - \tfrac1d$ is
not an integer and lies strictly between $M$ and $M+1$; so the condition is
exactly $L \ge M + 1$.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{(A5) Case P, inequality (E1).} Start from the sufficient form
\[
  2\big[ (a-1)(ra - M) + M - 1 \big] \;\le\; a r (M - r - 1) + 2a(r-1) .
\]
Left side: $2(a-1)ra - 2(a-1)M + 2M - 2 = 2ra^2 - 2ra - 2aM + 4M - 2$. Right
side: $arM - ar^2 - ar + 2ar - 2a = arM - ar^2 + ar - 2a$. Moving everything
right, the inequality is
\[
  0 \;\le\; arM + 2aM - 4M - ar^2 - 2ra^2 + 3ar - 2a + 2
  \;=\; M(ar + 2a - 4) - ar^2 - 2ra^2 + 3ar - 2a + 2 ,
\]
as displayed in the text. Now insert $2M \ge ra + 1$ (valid since $ar + 2a - 4 >
0$), multiplying the target by $2$:
\begin{align*}
  2M(ar + 2a - 4) &- 2ar^2 - 4ra^2 + 6ar - 4a + 4 \\
  &\ge (ra+1)(ar + 2a - 4) - 2ar^2 - 4ra^2 + 6ar - 4a + 4 \\
  &= \big[ a^2r^2 + 2a^2r - 4ar + ar + 2a - 4 \big] - 2ar^2 - 4ra^2 + 6ar - 4a + 4 \\
  &= a^2r^2 - 2a^2r - 2ar^2 + 3ar - 2a
   \;=\; a\big( ar^2 - 2ar - 2r^2 + 3r - 2 \big) .
\end{align*}
So (E1) holds whenever $ar^2 - 2ar - 2r^2 + 3r - 2 \ge 0$, i.e.\ whenever
$a\,r\,(r-2) \ge 2r^2 - 3r + 2$ --- the condition $(\ast)$ of the text.

%======================================================================
\section{Hints and answers for the exercises}\label{app:hints}

One entry per exercise, in reading order, titled by its section and topic.
Entries come in four kinds: a \emph{hint} leaves you work; an \emph{answer}
settles the matter; \textsc{challenge} marks something genuinely hard and safely
skipped; \textsc{discussion} marks an open-ended prompt with no single clean
answer --- an invitation to experiment, not a debt. If a exercise has parts,
the entry follows them.

\subsection*{Part I}

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:problem}, walks.} (a) Any aperiodic rule works, e.g.\ steps
$3,2,3,3,2,3,3,3,2,\dots$ (one $2$ after ever more $3$s); nothing obliges a walk
to repeat. (b) Sum the $n-1$ gap bounds $d_1 \le g_i \le d_2$.

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:problem}, sumsets.} (a) All members are odd, and sums of
three odd numbers are odd; the answer is every odd number from $3$ to $25$. (b)
Each summand is $\ge a_1$. (c) $k a_{n+1} - k a_n = k g_n \le k d_2$.

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:problem}, lacunary growth.} Induction: $b_i \ge r b_{i-1}$.
Then $b_1 r^{\,i-1} \le b_i \le N$ gives $i \le 1 + \log_r(N/b_1) \le 1 + \log_2
N$.

\textbf{Exercise~\ref{chk:lazy-ap}.} (a) $kA = \{kc + sd : s \ge k\}$
(Example~\ref{ex:steady-hopeless}); the Adversary picks $b_1 = kc + kd$, then
$b_{i+1}$ a far-later element of the same progression. (b) \textsc{challenge}
--- this is Part III's certificate trick; the honest answer at this stage is
``not obviously, since different progressions own different classes''.

\textbf{Exercise~\ref{chk:floors}.} Add $x_t - 1 < \lfloor x_t \rfloor \le
x_t$ over $t$.

\textbf{Exercise~\ref{chk:daylight35}.} (a) Only sums of terms $\le 19$
matter; there are finitely many, and none hits $18$ or $25$. (b) Any sum using a
term $> 25$ exceeds $25$. (c) $32$ and $39$ (the pattern is $18 + 7j$: daylight
recurs once per two strides here).

\textbf{Exercise~\ref{chk:s-range}.} $i_t \ge 1$ forces $s \ge k$; conversely
$i_1 = s - k + 1,\ i_2 = \cdots = i_k = 1$.

\subsection*{Part II}

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:beatty}, floors.} (a) $5$, $-3$, $-4$. (b) $x = y = 0.5$:
$\lfloor 1 \rfloor = 1 \ne 0$; in general write $x = \lfloor x \rfloor + \{x\}$
and split on whether $\{x\} + \{y\} < 1$. (c) $m + n$ is no longer an integer,
so the defining property does not transfer.

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:beatty}, Beatty walks.} (a) $\lfloor id \rfloor = id$. (b)
Gap $4$ exactly at odd $i$: $\lfloor (i+1)\cdot\tfrac72 \rfloor - \lfloor
i\cdot\tfrac72 \rfloor$ depends on the parity of $i$. (c) Telescope: the sum of
the first $n$ gaps is $\lfloor (n+1)\gamma \rfloor - \lfloor \gamma \rfloor$;
each gap is $\lfloor\gamma\rfloor$ or $\lfloor\gamma\rfloor + 1$, so the number
of big gaps is the total minus $n\lfloor\gamma\rfloor$.

\textbf{Exercise~\ref{chk:conservative}.} (a) $s = 8$: $3.5 \cdot 8 = 28 \in
[25, 28]$. (b) $28$ sits on the \emph{closed} right endpoint; the half-open
window means membership of $25$ in $3A$ would require $s\gamma \in [25,\ 28)$
for some $s$; the only candidate is $3.5 \cdot 8 = 28$, which the half-open
interval excludes --- so the dodge succeeds. The \emph{closed} certification
interval $[25, 28]$ does contain $28$, so the certificate fails: conservative,
not wrong. (c) The smallest multiple $u$ of $3.5$ with $u \ge t$ satisfies $u < t
+ 3.5$; multiples of $3.5$ are integers or half-integers, and $(t+3, t+3.5)$
contains neither, so $u \le t + 3$: every closed window is hit. (d) \textsc{answer.} The unsafe intervals meeting $(3,4)$ are exactly those with
$s = 12, \dots, 16$:
\[
  \Big[\tfrac{46}{12}, \tfrac{49}{12}\Big],\ \
  \Big[\tfrac{46}{13}, \tfrac{49}{13}\Big],\ \
  \Big[\tfrac{46}{14}, \tfrac{49}{14}\Big],\ \
  \Big[\tfrac{46}{15}, \tfrac{49}{15}\Big],\ \
  \Big[\tfrac{46}{16}, \tfrac{49}{16}\Big] ,
\]
i.e.\ approximately $[3.833, 4.083]$, $[3.538, 3.769]$, $[3.286, 3.500]$,
$[3.067, 3.267]$, $[2.875, 3.063]$. The certified slopes are what is left of
$(3,4)$ --- \emph{four} open gaps, not one:
\[
  \Big(\tfrac{49}{16}, \tfrac{46}{15}\Big),\quad
  \Big(\tfrac{49}{15}, \tfrac{46}{14}\Big),\quad
  \Big(\tfrac{49}{14}, \tfrac{46}{13}\Big),\quad
  \Big(\tfrac{49}{13}, \tfrac{46}{12}\Big) ,
\]
approximately $(3.063, 3.067)$, $(3.267, 3.286)$, $(3.500, 3.538)$ and
$(3.769, 3.833)$. The text's $\gamma = 3.8$ lies in the last and widest of them
--- and note how the gaps widen toward the right, exactly as
\S\ref{sec:freegap} exploits by aiming $s_1$ at the top of the interval.

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:windows}, $t = 20$.} (a) $s = 6, 7$ give $[10/3, 23/6]$ and
$[20/7, 23/7]$; all other unsafe intervals miss $(3,4)$. (b) Any $\gamma \in
(23/6, 4)$, e.g.\ $\gamma = 3.9$: then $b = 20 + 3c \notin 3A_{\gamma,c}$.

\textbf{Exercise~\ref{chk:overlap}.} Cross-multiply: $(t+k)s \ge t(s+1) \iff
ks \ge t$.

\textbf{Exercise~\ref{chk:freegap-concrete}.} (a) $10000 \ge 512$ and $0.5 \ge
0.0064$. (b) About $t/\gamma^2 \cdot |I| \approx 300$ of them. (c) $s_1 =
250000$, $J = (10^6{+}3)/250001 \dots 4$, width $= 1/250001$.

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:freegap}, hypotheses.} $t \ge 32 d_2^2$ enters at
\eqref{eq:s1-lower}, in the width chain (via $t/(32(d_2 - \tfrac12)) \ge d_2 +
\tfrac12$), and via the input-width hypothesis in check (iii). For small $t$
try $t = 20$, $d_2 = 4$: $J$ is wide but pokes below $lo$.

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:nested}, trace.} $j_0 = 1$ ($b_1 = 800 \ge 64$), $c = 801$,
$t_2 = 800^2 - 3 \cdot 801 = 637{,}597$; then $s_1 = \lceil t_2/(d_2 - \tfrac14)
\rceil$ etc.\ --- any slope in the resulting middle third qualifies.

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:nested}, eventual lacunarity.} Only the choice of $j_0$
changes: pick it past $i_0$ as well.

\subsection*{Part III}

\textbf{Exercise~\ref{chk:remainders-1}.} (a) Each step preserves common
divisors ($d \mid x, y \Rightarrow d \mid x - qy$); $\gcd(2026, 715) = \gcd(715,
596) = \gcd(596, 119) = \gcd(119, 1) = 1$. (b) $21 \cdot 5 - 8 \cdot 13 = 105 -
104 = 1$. (c) $8^{-1} \equiv 8 \pmod{21}$ ($64 = 63 + 1$); $s \equiv 8 \cdot 5 =
40 \equiv 19 \pmod{21}$.

\textbf{Exercise~\ref{chk:remainders-2}.} (a) $0,5,3,1,6,4,2$: all seven. (b)
$0, 6, 4, 2$ --- only the even classes. (c) Multiply by the inverse; solutions
$s \equiv \bar\alpha c$.

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:cert}, the $3,5$ example.} (a) Two gaps sum to $8$; a prefix
of the gap word is $m$ full $(3,5)$-blocks plus possibly a leading $3$. (b) Fix
all five indices even (contributing $8m_t$); ``sufficiently large'' is $5 a_1 +
8 \cdot 0$ onward within the class. (c) Sums are $\equiv 3j \pmod 8$ with $j \le
5$, and $2 \notin \{0,3,6,1,4,7\}$.

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:cert}, certificate values.} $b_2 = 11$, $b_3 = 1102$, $b_4 =
1{,}102{,}001$; ratios $11 \ge 10$, $1102/11 \ge 100$, etc.

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:periodic}, $(2,3,2)$.} Prefix sums modulo $7$ are $\{0, 2,
5\}$; sums of three of them hit all seven classes --- so $3A - 3a_1$ eventually
contains \emph{every} class modulo $7$, i.e.\ all large integers.

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:words}, balance.} (b): windows $00$ and $11$. Final claim:
compare both windows to the initial window of the same length.

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:widths}, $0011\cdots$.} $q = 0, 0, 1, 2, 2, 2, 3, 4, \dots$;
$\sigma_0 = 4$: $q_0 + q_4 = 2$, $q_2 + q_2 = 0$, so $W_2(4) = 2$. For $s = 12$:
configurations $(4,4,4)$ (value $6$) and $(2,2,8)$ (value $4$).

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:widths}, $W_2$ vs width.} Pad with $k - 2$ zero indices
($q_0 = 0$): a two-index spread survives into $k$ indices. Strict inequality:
almost any word with $k = 3$, e.g.\ $0011\cdots$ at $s = 12$ has width $\ge 3$.

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:sturmian-course}, examples.} (a) $\alpha = \tfrac13$; $D_n$
cycles through $0, -\tfrac13, -\tfrac23$ shifted... compute: $q_{3m} = m$, so
$D_{3m} = 0$; $w_n = 1$ for all $n$, and Step 4's argument ends immediately at
period $3$. (b) $\{j\alpha\}$ for $j = 0..5$: two land within $\tfrac15$;
Dirichlet gives $n_0 = 5$, $\theta = |5\sqrt2 - 7| \approx 0.071$, so $L = 5
\cdot 16 = 80$. \textsc{challenge} to tighten: the true minimal $L$ is far
smaller.

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:sturmian-course}, irrationality.} Once in ``at most one
exceptional index'' (two would give $(n_1 - n_2)\alpha \in \ZZ$), once in
$\alpha \notin \{0, 1\}$. For rational $\alpha$ the exceptional indices recur
periodically and the tail-pass fails --- consistent with Step 4 taking over.

\textbf{Exercise~\ref{chk:fib-verify}.} $q_{10} = 4$, $q_{26} = 10$, $q_{36} =
14$: sums $72$ and $28$. Another valid triple: $(10, 23, 39)$, with $4 + 9 + 15
= 28$.

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:sturmian-endgame}, why not the sweep.} \textsc{challenge}.
$W_s$ sits inside a real window of length $k$ (Step 0), so $\mathrm{width}(s)
\le k - 1$ always; for a Sturmian word the two extreme integers of the window
are attained only when fractional parts pile up near $0$ (resp.\ near $1$)
\emph{simultaneously}, which balancedness forbids infinitely often --- the band
is genuinely too narrow at the corner $\delta' = d_2 = k$.

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:slot}, small $m$'s.} (a) $\{1, 2\}$: sums $0,1,2,3$;
$\{2,3,4\}$: sums $0,2,3,4,5,6,7,9 \supseteq [2,7]$. (b) Use $S = \{2,3,4\}$
from (a): its sums cover $[2,7]$, amply containing $M = 4$ consecutive values.
Slots at three positions with gaps $2, 3, 4$ inside the first two periods;
nothing to park ($k - 1 - m = 0$); dial $\phi$ down by steps $\le 4$ into the
block.

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:slot}, parking and leaping.} Parking at $p_0 = 0$ is legal
(indices $\ge 0$ are allowed in Lemma~\ref{lem:kA-prefix}) and changes only
$\mathrm{base}$. Leap example: block $\{4, 5\}$ ($M = 3$ would need length $3$),
dial multiples of $3$: $\phi$ passes $6 \to 3$, skipping both.

\subsection*{Part IV}

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:sharp-intro}, small cases.} (a) Answer: $m(\{1,2,M\}) =
\lceil M/2 \rceil$. Construction: one $1$ and $j := \lceil (M-2)/2 \rceil$ twos
--- their sums fill $[\,0,\ 2j+1\,]$ solidly (twos reach the even numbers, the
$1$ adjusts parity), a run of $2j + 2 \ge M$, at size $1 + j = \lceil M/2
\rceil$. The matching lower bound is a small \textsc{challenge}: show a run of
$M$ consecutive sums needs both parities (hence the $1$, for even $M$) and total
reach $\ge M - 1$ from the $1$s and $2$s alone --- copies of $M$ cannot glue a
run longer than the $\{1,2\}$-part supplies. (b) \textsc{discussion}, genuinely
open-ended: $2^s \ge M$ forces $s \ge \log_2 M$, but with only three generators
the sums form a rigid three-parameter grid; experiment with $G = \{a, M-1, M\}$
and watch $\min G$ control how far below $M - 1$ the truth sits. No clean
closed answer is intended here.

\textbf{Exercise~\ref{chk:twogen}.} Coprimality enters choosing $j$ with
$j\beta \equiv n$; for $(2,4)$ all grid values are even --- no odd number is
ever covered, and no interval of length $\ge 2$ either.

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:sharp-machinery}, $(5,7,9)$ and $\{5^{(x)}, 7\}$.} The sums
of $x$ fives and one seven occupy only residues $\{0, 2\}$ modulo $5$: no five
consecutive integers, for any $x$. Missing ingredient: a box covering \emph{all}
residues.

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:sharp-machinery}, the lift's hypothesis.} \textsc{answer.}
$Y + Z \le a - 1$ caps the budget growth at $Y + Z + 1 \le a$ per lift, matched
by the target's growth of exactly $a$. A concrete instance of the failure: take
$a = 5$, and a box with $Y + Z = 5 = a$, say $(Y, Z) = (3, 2)$, on the class base
$(5, 16, 17)$. The representative heights are now bounded by $S_0 = 3 \cdot 16 +
2 \cdot 17 = 82$ rather than $34$, so the budget is $\lceil (16 + 82)/5 \rceil +
5 = 20 + 5 = 25$ against a target $M - 1 = 16$ --- already failing, and the
lift makes it worse: at $(5, 21, 22)$ the same box gives $S_0 = 3 \cdot 21 + 2
\cdot 22 = 107$, budget $\lceil (21+107)/5 \rceil + 5 = 26 + 5 = 31$ against
target $21$. The budget rose by $6 = Y + Z + 1$, the target by $5 = a$: the
margin lost one unit on the step, and will lose one on every step forever. With
$Y + Z \le a - 1$ that leak is exactly plugged.

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:sharp-reduction}, routing.} $(5,12,15)$: D. $(3,4,5)$: P.
$(5,7,9)$: L. $(13,22,27)$: E. $(5,8,9)$: T. $(5,16,17)$: B. And $(12, 25,
36)$ is not hard-core: $a + b = 37 = M + 1$, so Lemma~\ref{lem:L2-friendly}
catches it.

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:sharp-reduction}, smallest members.} $(a,\ a+1,\ a+2)$ is
hard-core for every $a \ge 3$ ($\gcd(a, a+1) = 1$, $h = 1 \le a - 2$); nothing
smaller than $(3,4,5)$ exists since $a \ge 3$ and $M \ge a + 2$.

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:caseDL}, Case L on $(5,7,9)$.} $(x, y, z) = (5, 1, 2)$:
budget $8 = M - 1$. Levels $c = 3$ through $c + g = 5$ cover solidly; the
difference from $\{5,5,5,7\}$ is exactly the extra frames that make the merge
condition true.

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:caseP-friendly}, $(3,4,5)$.} $\mathrm{low} = \max(1 \cdot 4,\
1 \cdot 5) = 5$; targets $5, \dots, 9$; e.g.\ $8 = 4 + 4$? No --- follow the
branches: $8 \equiv 2 \pmod 3$, $w = 2 > n_b = 1$, so $v = 1$: $8 = 5 + 3$. The
mover appears for target $9 = 4 + 5$.

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:caseP-friendly}, (E1) derivation.} Mechanical; item (A5)
shows the target form.

\textbf{Exercise~\ref{chk:caseE}.} Answer, on the concrete triple $(13, 24,
34)$, which routes to E with $\eta = 9$ (check: $\bar e = 11$, $\bar\mu = 8$,
$11^{-1} \equiv 6$, $\eta = 48 \bmod 13 = 9$). Working from $\eta$ directly
would take the box $(8, 1)$: top height $8 \cdot 24 + 34 = 226$, budget $\lceil
(33 + 226)/13 \rceil + 9 = 20 + 9 = 29$. Working from $t = \min(9, 4) = 4$
takes the box $(3, 3)$: top height $3 \cdot 24 + 3 \cdot 34 = 174$, budget
$\lceil (33 + 174)/13 \rceil + 6 = 16 + 6 = 22$. Both pass here ($M - 1 = 33$),
but the $\min$ is systematically cheaper --- and it is what keeps $\sigma \le
a/2 + 2$, which the general threshold argument needs. Final claim: $2 + (a-1)/2
= (a+3)/2$: multiply out.

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:caseT-friendly}, $(7,9,10)$.} $y = 2$, $z = 2$, $x = 5$:
multiset $\{7^5, 9^2, 10^2\}$, nine elements; levels $4$ and $5$ cover $[28 +
C,\ 35 + V]$ with the merge exact; budget $9 = M - 1$.

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:caseT-friendly}, $(5,8,9)$.} Construction budget $11 > 8$
(the line $(3,1)$ is unlucky at $a = 5$); the replacement certificate is checked
by listing $8j + 9k$ residues $\{0,3,1,4,2\}$ and the run $[17, 25]$.

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:caseB-friendly}, the base and one lift.} Residues of $16j +
17k$: $\{0, 1, 2, 3, 4\}$; heights $\le 50$; budget $13$. At $(5, 21, 22)$ the
exact budget is $16$ (recompute $S = 44$, $x = 13$) --- better than the lemma's
guaranteed $13 + 4 = 17$.

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:sharp-assembly}, overlaps.} Answer, and a pleasing one:
both branches produce the \emph{same} multiset. Case L at $(3,4,5)$ has $a = 3$,
$g = 1$, so $(x, y, z) = (\lfloor \tfrac12 \rfloor + 2,\ 1,\ \lceil \tfrac12
\rceil) = (2, 1, 1)$ --- the multiset $\{3, 3, 4, 5\}$, exactly Case P's
witness. The two mechanisms converge at the smallest triple. D-and-E-sizes
example: $(13, 25, 26)$ ($a \mid M$, both sizes $\ge 12$); the router reads
conditions in order, so only D fires --- overlap costs nothing.

\subsection*{Part V}

\textbf{\S\ref{sec:assembly-friendly}, grand tour.} \textsc{challenge}, and the
best single exercise in the document. Route: two letters $\{1, 2\}$, aperiodic
balanced (Fibonacci) $\to$ Sturmian $\to$ modulus $m = 1$ from
Lemma~\ref{lem:sturmian-friendly}; the certificate meets $kA$ at any listed pair
$(1, 0)$ whose $b_i$ clears the walk's threshold $X_0$.

\section{The certificate tables}\label{app:tables}

These are the $172 + 178$ finite certificates that Cases~T and~B consume ---
the entire finite layer of the proof, printed in full so that this paper is
self-contained.

Each entry is a triple $(a,b,M)$ together with a \emph{box certificate}
$(x,Y,Z)$, asserting that the multiset $S$ of $x$ copies of $a$, $Y$ copies of
$b$ and $Z$ copies of $M$ satisfies
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{budget:} $x + Y + Z \le M - 1$, and
\item \textbf{coverage:} $\ssum(S)$ contains $M$ consecutive integers,
\end{itemize}
i.e.\ that $S$ witnesses $m(G) \le M-1$ for $G = \{a,b,M\}$. The mechanism is
the frame lemma (Lemma~\ref{lem:frame-friendly}): the copies of $b$ and $M$
realize a modulo-$a$ box covering every residue, and the $x$ copies of $a$ lift
that residue cover to a solid run.

\emph{You can check any row of these tables by hand in a few minutes}, and that
is the point of printing them. Take the row $(5,8,9) \to (5,2,1)$, which is
Exercise~\ref{chk:caseT-cert}'s worked instance: the multiset is $\{5,5,5,5,5,\,
8,8,\, 9\}$, of size $5+2+1 = 8 = M-1$, meeting the budget exactly. Its parts
$8j + 9k$ with $j \le 2$, $k \le 1$ take the values $\{0, 8, 16, 9, 17\}$, whose
residues modulo $5$ are $\{0, 3, 1, 4, 2\}$ --- all five. Lifting by the copies
of $5$ gives the run $[17, 25]$, which is $9 = M$ consecutive integers. That
single computation is exactly what the checker performs on each row, and what
the Lean kernel re-decides.

These tables are \emph{generated}, not transcribed: \texttt{gen-tables.py}
parses the canonical source \texttt{data/certificate-data.md}, re-verifies every
row's budget and coverage by exact arbitrary-precision bitmask, aborts the build
on any failure, and only then emits the \LaTeX{} below. The same rows are
exported as CSV with recorded digests and transcribed into Lean, where the
kernel decides them (\S\ref{sec:computer}). No listing --- printed, exported, or
kernel-checked --- can silently diverge from the others.

\input{table-a.tex}

\input{table-b.tex}

\section{Correspondence with the Lean development}\label{app:lean}

Declarations live in the namespace \texttt{Erdos1112.Proof}, in files relative to
\texttt{Erdos1112Proof/}, except the three headline theorems, which are in
namespace \texttt{Erdos1112}. Every listed result is load-bearing: the development
formalizes the full case tree rather than citing anything externally. The
name-and-location layer of this table is checked mechanically
(\texttt{scripts/check\_correspondence.py}, re-run in continuous integration), so
it cannot silently rot; the \emph{statement-level} match --- that each declaration
says what the prose result says --- remains a human judgement, and is where we
invite scrutiny.

One row is marked \textsc{alt}: for Case~P this paper gives a single uniform
argument for the whole line (\S\ref{sec:caseP-friendly}) while the formalization
instantiates small $r$ separately and routes the rest through an auxiliary lemma.
The formal statement proved is the one the assembly consumes; it is not weaker.
